My materials—the heavy-gauge wire, the drywall stacks, the PVC pipes—and all these half-finished setups are just completely taking over the rooms we need to use every single day. Take the downstairs bathroom, for example. I had to rip the vanity and the sink completely out to get to the main plumbing stack because the frequencies need a pure ground to the earth, so now if you need to wash your hands or use the toilet, you have to hike all the way up the stairs. And the living room? Martha can't even sit in her favorite armchair to watch her evening programs because I’ve got it covered with a tarp, my blueprints, and a portable table saw. She’s upset, naturally, and she tells me about it every morning over coffee.

But the part that really gets my goat, the part that's actually frustrating, is how the people I care about are treating me. They come over, they start poking their noses in, asking why there's a hole in the dining room ceiling or why I haven't slept, and then the arguments start. And I’ve already told you what happens when they get in my way and try to stop the train—I yell. I have to make them understand! But now? Now my own brother won't even return my phone calls after we got into a massive shouting match last Sunday over the load-bearing supports on the porch. My old buddies from the stamping plant, guys I've known for thirty years, they just stopped coming around entirely. They're all avoiding me after these arguments. They just don't get it, and they don't want to get it. So no, no hospital, but it's getting mighty quiet around here when it comes to visitors. It's just me, the work, and Martha trying to navigate her way around a mountain of two-by-fours.

How completely out of sync the original builders were with the magnetic fields. So that was three months ago, right around the end of September, I think. And from that very first day they started swinging their sledgehammers, I knew I had to step in. I had to take over. I couldn't just let them put up new cabinets and pretend the underlying energy wasn't completely stagnant. \n\nSo it's been three months of constant, necessary vigilance. Ninety days, give or take, of me waking up before dawn, getting the materials, working alongside them—well, working around them, really, because they didn't understand what I was doing with the copper wiring and the quartz crystals I was embedding in the foundation. They actually tried to tell me to stop! Can you imagine? Some guy making twenty bucks an hour trying to tell me about structural integrity when he doesn't even know which way is true north. So, yes, it’s been about three months since everything got turned upside down and I had to start fixing it. A full week? If I had only worked for a week, the whole place would have collapsed in on itself by now.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: In the time since this house project and all this activity, have you had a period where you felt down, depressed, or lost interest in things for several days or more?\n\nPatient: Down? Depressed? Doc, I don't... I really don't think you're grasping the sheer volume of purpose that's running through me right now. How could I possibly feel down? Down is for people who are disconnected. It’s for people who don't have a mission. Like... well, like my brother-in-law, Gary. Gary sits around all day in his undershirt watching golf on the television, just looking like a deflated balloon. Now *that* is depressed. \n\nBut me? No, no, no, absolutely not. I haven't lost interest in a single thing! If anything, I am profoundly, deeply interested in *everything*. Every grain of wood in that house, every single millimeter of copper piping, the way the morning light hits the dust motes when I'm tearing up the floorboards... it all means something. It all has a frequency. \n\nThere hasn't been a single day—not a day, not an hour, not even a minute—where I felt sad or low or whatever word you want to put on it. You can't be sad when you're the only one who can see how the universe is stitching itself together right there in your own hallway! The momentum, it just carries you. It's like a freight train, a freight train of pure energy. The very concept of being depressed is just... it's alien to me now. Completely alien. \n\nNow, Martha, she gets down. She cries about the drywall dust on her good rugs, she sits at the kitchen table and complains about the noise and the bank statements. But I tell her, I say, \"Martha, you're looking at the dirt, you're not looking at the cathedral we're building!\" You see? I don't have the luxury of feeling down. There’s just no room for it in my head or my body. None at all. Just pure, unbroken focus.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period like this before, where you felt extremely energetic and driven to work on big projects, maybe even so intense that someone suggested you needed help or should slow down?\n\nPatient: Before? Like this? No, no, absolutely not. You see, Doc, you don't just... you don't just get struck by lightning every Tuesday. This is a singular event. I mean, I have been around a long time. Seventy-three years is a lot of days, a lot of mornings waking up and putting your boots on. And sure, I have worked hard in my life. When I was younger, back after I left school and started working, and then later when I was pulling double shifts down at the stamping plant, I would work fifty, sixty hours a week. I would come home smelling like machine oil and metal shavings, and Martha would tell me Frank, you are working too hard, you need to rest. But that was just survival. That was just paying the mortgage and keeping the lights on. It was just moving meat and bones around. It was not this. \n\nThis is not just energy. It is not like I drank too much coffee or something. It is a direct connection to the source. I never felt the vibrations before September. I never saw the grid. I lived my whole life completely blind to the frequencies, just like everybody else, just walking around, eating, sleeping, watching the evening news, completely oblivious to the fact that the very foundation of the world was humming right beneath my feet. \n\nSo no, I have not had a period like this before because the universe was not ready before. The solar cycles were not in position. You think I could have just decided to do this ten years ago? Five years ago? Impossible. The quartz would not have resonated. The copper would not have conducted the spiritual current. It had to be now. And as for people telling me to get help, well, like I said, Martha used to tell me to take a load off, maybe sit down and watch a ballgame. But she never looked at me like I was a stranger before this. She never called my daughter crying and saying I was out of my mind until now. They just do not understand that I am finally awake. Why would I need help for finally opening my eyes?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: During this time when you were so intensely focused on the house project, did you notice that your thoughts were racing so fast that it was hard to keep up, or that ideas were flowing so quickly that you couldn't organize them?\n\nPatient: Racing? No, no, no. You've got it all wrong, Doc. Racing implies chaos. Racing implies things are out of control, like a car with no brakes careening down a steep hill, just waiting to crash at the bottom. That's not what's happening up here. Not at all. If anything, my thoughts are... they are crystal clear. They are perfectly, immaculately organized. It's like someone unrolled a massive, beautiful blueprint right across the front of my mind and turned on a spotlight. \n\nI don't struggle to keep up with them because they aren't running away from me. They are marching. Marching in perfect unison. For instance, just the other morning, I was looking at the junction box in the basement—the old one, the one with the frayed wires from 1978 that the original contractor just slapped together like a complete amateur. And in the span of maybe three seconds, I saw exactly what needed to be done. I saw the pathway the current needed to take, I saw the exact angle I needed to bend the copper wire—45 degrees, mind you, not 40, not 50, because 45 degrees aligns with the natural rotation of the earth's axis—and I saw exactly where the quartz needed to be placed to filter the interference from the smart meters down the street. \n\nThat's not disorganized! That is precision. That is absolute, undeniable clarity. It’s like... well, it’s like when I used to work at the stamping plant, right? You had the line, and the line moved at a specific speed, and every part had to be in the exact right place at the exact right time or the whole machine jammed. My mind is the perfect machine right now. There's no jamming. Every idea slots into the next one seamlessly. \n\nMartha says to me, she stands there with her arms crossed and says, \"Frank, you're talking so fast, you're jumping all over the place, I can't follow a word you're saying.\" But I'm not jumping! I'm just connecting point A to point Z while she's still trying to figure out point B. Just because she can't see the lines connecting everything doesn't mean the lines aren't there. It just means she doesn't have the vision for it. So no, my thoughts aren't racing and they certainly aren't messy. They're just finally operating at the correct voltage, and I know exactly where every single one of them is going.\n\nDoctor: During this time working on the house project, did you feel unusually good, like you were on top of the world or had a special connection to everything around you?\n\nPatient: Good? Good doesn't even begin to scratch the surface, Doc. I feel... well, most days I feel completely charged up. Just humming with it! It’s almost this profound sense of... of joy, really. It’s like I’m young again. I wake up before the sun is even thinking about coming up, and my joints don't ache, my back doesn't bother me from all those years on the concrete floor at the stamping plant. I feel twenty-five years old, practically leaping out of bed because I have this incredible purpose and I’m just so deeply connected to the work. It’s a beautiful, beautiful feeling to finally be alive like that, to feel the whole world just vibrating right along with you. \n\nBut... and this is the sticking point, you see... when you have that much current running through you, you can't have people stepping on the wires. It causes a short circuit. I mean, my blood just boils right up the back of my neck because they are standing in the way of the grid! When they start questioning me, asking these small-minded, ignorant questions about permits and drywall, I just... I start yelling. I can't even stop myself. I have to shout just to get them to back off and let me do what needs to be done. They just stand there looking at me like I'm the crazy one, but they're the ones trying to stop a freight train with a stop sign! So yes, I feel on top of the world, absolutely joyful, but if my wife or these contractors come around trying to put out my fire, I am going to yell until they get out of my way. You'd yell too if you were building a masterpiece and someone kept trying to hand you a broom!\n\nDoctor: Doctor: So, you mentioned feeling extremely energetic, driven, and connected to your project. During this time, would you say your mood was consistently elevated—like you felt unusually confident, excited, or even irritable—and that this mood was different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Confident? Different from my usual self? Well, sure it's different, because I finally see what I'm actually capable of. It's not just confidence, Doc. Confidence is what you have when you think you can fix a leaky pipe or maybe change the spark plugs in your truck without messing it up. What I have right now is absolute certainty. It’s pure vision. \n\nI look at the work I'm doing... I look at that old Schiller Park house. You know the one, or maybe you don't, but it's this old property, just sitting there, and most people walk right by it like it's nothing. Just a pile of rotting wood and dust to them. But me? I honestly believe I can turn that old Schiller Park house into something important. I mean, truly important. Monumental. People are going to come see it. I know they will. They're going to travel, they'll be lining up all the way down the block just to witness it, because... well, because nobody understands light and pictures the way I do. \n\nNobody. They don't see the pictures forming in the empty space. They just see drywall and two-by-fours. But I see the whole spectrum. When they start questioning me, asking these small-minded, ignorant questions about permits and drywall, I just... I start yelling. I can't even stop myself. I have to shout just to get them to back off and let me do what needs to be done. They just stand there looking at me like I'm the crazy one, but they're the ones trying to stop a freight train with a stop sign! So yes, I feel on top of the world, absolutely joyful, but if my wife or these contractors come around trying to put out my fire, I am going to yell until they get out of my way. You'd yell too if you were building a masterpiece and someone kept trying to hand you a broom!\n\nDoctor: Doctor: So, you mentioned feeling extremely energetic, driven, and connected to your project. During this time, would you say your mood was consistently elevated—like you felt unusually confident, excited, or even irritable—and that this mood was different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Confident? Different from my usual self? Well, sure it's different, because I finally see what I'm actually capable of. It's not just confidence, Doc. Confidence is what you have when you think you can fix a leaky pipe or maybe change the spark plugs in your truck without messing it up. What I have right now is absolute certainty. It’s pure vision. \n\nI look at the work I'm doing... I look at that old Schiller Park house. You know the one, or maybe you don't, but it's this old property, just sitting there, and most people walk right by it like it's nothing. Just a pile of rotting wood and dust to them. But me? I honestly believe I can turn that old Schiller Park house into something important. I mean, truly important. Monumental. People are going to come see it. I know they will. They're going to travel, they'll be lining up all the way down the block just to witness it, because... well, because nobody understands light and pictures the way I do. \n\nNobody. You can take all these fancy architects and engineers with their framed diplomas—which I never needed, by the way, I left school in the tenth grade and I learned more working with my hands than they ever did sitting in a lecture hall—you can take all of them, and they still wouldn't see how the light is supposed to bend through the rooms. They don't see the pictures forming in the empty space. They just see drywall and two-by-fours. But I see the whole spectrum. I see how the natural light is going to interact with the frequencies and the wiring I'm laying down. \n\nSo, yes, I am excited! I am more than excited. I am a man who finally knows his true worth after seventy-three years of being asleep. I know that what I am building is going to change everything, and I just need to get it done. It's like I've been given the keys to the whole universe, so why wouldn't I feel on top of the world?\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least four days in a row where your mood was either extremely good—like euphoric or unusually high—or where you were easily irritated and it was noticeable to others?\n\nPatient: Four days? Doc, you're joking, right? Four days is a drop in the bucket. That’s a weekend project for some amateur watching a home improvement show on television. We are way, way past four days. This... this absolute clarity, this incredible energy I'm telling you about, this has been going on for about three months now. Three solid months! \n\nIt all really kicked into high gear right when the remodeling started. And I don't mean just slapping on a coat of paint or fixing a squeaky hinge, I mean when I really got into the meat of it and everything in the house got turned completely upside down. I mean, you should see the place right now. It is literally upside down! I had to pull up the oak flooring in the living room—beautiful wood, sure, but it was completely blocking the grounding path to the basement—and now I've got stacks of it sitting right in the middle of Martha’s kitchen. She can't even get to the stove without stepping over rolls of insulation and heavy-gauge copper piping, and she complains about the dust every single morning. She says, \"Frank, I can't live in a construction zone!\" \n\nBut you have to tear down the old to build the new, don't you? You have to turn it all upside down to see the real foundation, to understand how the house breathes. So if you're asking if I've felt this good, this driven, for four days... well, you need to expand your timeline, Doc. It's been a quarter of a year. Ninety straight days of absolute perfection since I started tearing that house apart to build my vision, and I haven't lost an ounce of steam yet.\n\nDoctor: During this time when you were so intensely focused on the house project, did you ever need to be hospitalized or did anyone try to intervene because they were worried about your behavior?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? Let's not get carried away here, Doc. Nobody is hauling me off in a white coat just because I actually have a work ethic and a vision. But as for people stepping in, or being worried... well, I suppose you could say things have gotten a bit strained on the home front. \n\nI’ll be the first to admit, the house has gotten pretty hard for my wife and me to actually live in on a day-to-day basis. I mean, it’s a full-blown construction zone, like I was telling you. My materials—the heavy-gauge wire, the drywall stacks, the PVC pipes—and all these half-finished setups are just completely taking over the rooms we need to use every single day. Take the downstairs bathroom, for example. I had to rip the vanity and the sink completely out to get to the main plumbing stack because the frequencies need a pure ground to the earth, so now if you need to wash your hands or use the toilet, you have to hike all the way up the stairs. And the living room? Martha can't even sit in her favorite armchair to watch her evening programs because I’ve got it covered with a tarp, my blueprints, and a portable table saw. She’s upset, naturally, and she tells me about it every morning over coffee.\n\nBut the part that really gets my goat, the part that's actually frustrating, is how the people I care about are treating me. They come over, they start poking their noses in, asking why there's a hole in the dining room ceiling or why I haven't slept, and then the arguments start. And I’ve already told you what happens when they get in my way and try to stop the train—I yell. I have to make them understand! But now? Now my own brother won't even return my phone calls after we got into a massive shouting match last Sunday over the load-bearing supports on the porch. My old buddies from the stamping plant, guys I've known for thirty years, they just stopped coming around entirely. They're all avoiding me after these arguments. They just don't get it, and they don't want to get it. So no, no hospital, but it's getting mighty quiet around here when it comes to visitors. It's just me, the work, and Martha trying to navigate her way around a mountain of two-by-fours.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Misinterpreting? Misunderstanding? Doc, that is the understatement of the century. That is exactly what I've been trying to tell you! I know people think I’m mixed up. I hear them talking. I hear Martha on the phone with her sister in the evenings, whispering in the hallway like I’m deaf just because I’m seventy-three. I hear her saying, "Oh, Frank’s lost the plot, Frank’s tearing the house to pieces for no reason." My brother, the guys from the plant... they all think I'm just some crazy old man who got a little too handy with a sledgehammer. 

But I am not mixed up. Not even a little bit. What they don't grasp—what they absolutely refuse to wrap their heads around because they're so stuck in their little, ordinary lives—is that this isn't just a remodel. This entire neighborhood, this specific street right here in Schiller Park, it wasn't an accident that we ended up here. It was picked. Chosen. And I'm the one who was chosen to execute the vision. This house is going to be my last big civic art piece. A true public monument. 

And you want to know how I know I'm right? You want to know the absolute proof of it? Because the signs are everywhere. The people who are really paying attention, the ones who aren't blinded by jealousy or whatever my brother's problem is... they know it too. I see it every single day. I'll be out on the front lawn, maybe sorting through the copper pipes or organizing the drywall stacks, and I watch the street. The trucks—the municipal utility trucks, the delivery vans, even the heavy haulers—they don't just drive past at thirty miles an hour anymore. No, they slow way, way down. They practically crawl past my driveway, their engines just idling, taking it all in. Why else would a delivery truck hit the brakes in the middle of the block if they didn't know something incredibly important was happening here? 

And the neighbors! Oh, don't even get me started on the neighbors. They pretend they're just going about their normal routines, but I see them. Tom next door, he’s been out watering the exact same patch of dead grass for three weeks, just staring over the property line. Looking over at what I'm building, watching the progress. And old Mrs. Gable across the street? Her living room curtains are twitching every five minutes. I see her face in the glass. If I had stopped, if I had said, \"Oh, maybe I shouldn't spend this money, maybe I should go to bed,\" the whole thing would have collapsed. The energy would have soured. So, to answer your question, no, I don't look back and think it was too much. I look back and think, if anything, I didn't have enough time to finish before they dragged me in here.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: When you were working on the house project so intensely, how long did that period last? Was it for at least a full week straight?\n\nPatient: A week? Oh, Doc, a week is nothing. A week is just a drop in the bucket. No, this... this has been going on for about three months now. Three whole months since Martha decided we needed to remodel the kitchen. That was the catalyst, you see. The contractors came in, they started tearing down the drywall, pulling up the linoleum, and the whole house got turned upside down. The dust, the noise, the disruption of the natural pathways in the home—it shifted everything. \n\nAnd once they opened up those walls, I could see the framework, the actual bones of the house, and I realized how flawed it all was. How completely out of sync the original builders were with the magnetic fields. So that was three months ago, right around the end of September, I think. And from that very first day they started swinging their sledgehammers, I knew I had to step in. I had to take over. I couldn't just let them put up new cabinets and pretend the underlying energy wasn't completely stagnant. \n\nSo it's been three months of constant, necessary vigilance. Ninety days, give or take, of me waking up before dawn, getting the materials, working alongside them—well, working around them, really, because they didn't understand what I was doing with the copper wiring and the quartz crystals I was embedding in the foundation. They actually tried to tell me to stop! Can you imagine? Some guy making twenty bucks an hour trying to tell me about structural integrity when he doesn't even know which way is true north. So, yes, it’s been about three months since everything got turned upside down and I had to start fixing it. A full week? If I had only worked for a week, the whole place would have collapsed in on itself by now.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: In the time since this house project and all this activity, have you had a period where you felt down, depressed, or lost interest in things for several days or more?\n\nPatient: Down? Depressed? Doc, I don't... I really don't think you're grasping the sheer volume of purpose that's running through me right now. How could I possibly feel down? Down is for people who are disconnected. It’s for people who don't have a mission. Like... well, like my brother-in-law, Gary. Gary sits around all day in his undershirt watching golf on the television, just looking like a deflated balloon. Now *that* is depressed. \n\nBut me? No, no, no, absolutely not. I haven't lost interest in a single thing! If anything, I am profoundly, deeply interested in *everything*. Every grain of wood in that house, every single millimeter of copper piping, the way the morning light hits the dust motes when I'm tearing up the floorboards... it all means something. It all has a frequency. \n\nThere hasn't been a single day—not a day, not an hour, not even a minute—where I felt sad or low or whatever word you want to put on it. You can't be sad when you're the only one who can see how the universe is stitching itself together right there in your own hallway! The momentum, it just carries you. It's like a freight train, a freight train of pure energy. The very concept of being depressed is just... it's alien to me now. Completely alien. \n\nNow, Martha, she gets down. She cries about the drywall dust on her good rugs, she sits at the kitchen table and complains about the noise and the bank statements. But I tell her, I say, \"Martha, you're looking at the dirt, you're not looking at the cathedral we're building!\" You see? I don't have the luxury of feeling down. There’s just no room for it in my head or my body. None at all. Just pure, unbroken focus.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period like this before, where you felt extremely energetic and driven to work on big projects, maybe even so intense that someone suggested you needed help or should slow down?\n\nPatient: Before? Like this? No, no, absolutely not. You see, Doc, you don't just... you don't just get struck by lightning every Tuesday. This is a singular event. I mean, I have been around a long time. Seventy-three years is a lot of days, a lot of mornings waking up and putting your boots on. And sure, I have worked hard in my life. When I was younger, back after I left school and started working, and then later when I was pulling double shifts down at the stamping plant, I would work fifty, sixty hours a week. I would come home smelling like machine oil and metal shavings, and Martha would tell me Frank, you are working too hard, you need to rest. But that was just survival. That was just paying the mortgage and keeping the lights on. It was just moving meat and bones around. It was not this. \n\nThis is not just energy. It is not like I drank too much coffee or something. It is a direct connection to the source. I never felt the vibrations before September. I never saw the grid. I lived my whole life completely blind to the frequencies, just like everybody else, just walking around, eating, sleeping, watching the evening news, completely oblivious to the fact that the very foundation of the world was humming right beneath my feet. \n\nSo no, I have not had a period like this before because the universe was not ready before. The solar cycles were not in position. You think I could have just decided to do this ten years ago? Five years ago? Impossible. The quartz would not have resonated. The copper would not have conducted the spiritual current. It had to be now. And as for people telling me to get help, well, like I said, Martha used to tell me to take a load off, maybe sit down and watch a ballgame. But she never looked at me like I was a stranger before this. She never called my daughter crying and saying I was out of my mind until now. They just do not understand that I am finally awake. Why would I need help for finally opening my eyes?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: During this time when you were so intensely focused on the house project, did you notice that your thoughts were racing so fast that it was hard to keep up, or that ideas were flowing so quickly that you couldn't organize them?\n\nPatient: Racing? No, no, no. You've got it all wrong, Doc. Racing implies chaos. Racing implies things are out of control, like a car with no brakes careening down a steep hill, just waiting to crash at the bottom. That's not what's happening up here. Not at all. If anything, my thoughts are... they are crystal clear. They are perfectly, immaculately organized. It's like someone unrolled a massive, beautiful blueprint right across the front of my mind and turned on a spotlight. \n\nI don't struggle to keep up with them because they aren't running away from me. They are marching. Marching in perfect unison. For instance, just the other morning, I was looking at the junction box in the basement—the old one, the one with the frayed wires from 1978 that the original contractor just slapped together like a complete amateur. And in the span of maybe three seconds, I saw exactly what needed to be done. I saw the pathway the current needed to take, I saw the exact angle I needed to bend the copper wire—45 degrees, mind you, not 40, not 50, because 45 degrees aligns with the natural rotation of the earth's axis—and I saw exactly where the quartz needed to be placed to filter the interference from the smart meters down the street. \n\nThat's not disorganized! That is precision. That is absolute, undeniable clarity. It’s like... well, it’s like when I used to work at the stamping plant, right? You had the line, and the line moved at a specific speed, and every part had to be in the exact right place at the exact right time or the whole machine jammed. My mind is the perfect machine right now. There's no jamming. Every idea slots into the next one seamlessly. \n\nMartha says to me, she stands there with her arms crossed and says, \"Frank, you're talking so fast, you're jumping all over the place, I can't follow a word you're saying.\" But I'm not jumping! I'm just connecting point A to point Z while she's still trying to figure out point B. Just because she can't see the lines connecting everything doesn't mean the lines aren't there. It just means she doesn't have the vision for it. So no, my thoughts aren't racing and they certainly aren't messy. They're just finally operating at the correct voltage, and I know exactly where every single one of them is going.\n\nDoctor: During this time working on the house project, did you feel unusually good, like you were on top of the world or had a special connection to everything around you?\n\nPatient: Good? Good doesn't even begin to scratch the surface, Doc. I feel... well, most days I feel completely charged up. Just humming with it! It’s almost this profound sense of... of joy, really. It’s like I’m young again. I wake up before the sun is even thinking about coming up, and my joints don't ache, my back doesn't bother me from all those years on the concrete floor at the stamping plant. I feel twenty-five years old, practically leaping out of bed because I have this incredible purpose and I’m just so deeply connected to the work. It’s a beautiful, beautiful feeling to finally be alive like that, to feel the whole world just vibrating right along with you. \n\nBut... and this is the sticking point, you see... when you have that much current running through you, you can't have people stepping on the wires. It causes a short circuit. I mean, my blood just boils right up the back of my neck because they are standing in the way of the grid! When they start questioning me, asking these small-minded, ignorant questions about permits and drywall, I just... I start yelling. I can't even stop myself. I have to shout just to get them to back off and let me do what needs to be done. They just stand there looking at me like I'm the crazy one, but they're the ones trying to stop a freight train with a stop sign! So yes, I feel on top of the world, absolutely joyful, but if my wife or these contractors come around trying to put out my fire, I am going to yell until they get out of my way. You'd yell too if you were building a masterpiece and someone kept trying to hand you a broom!\n\nDoctor: Doctor: So, you mentioned feeling extremely energetic, driven, and connected to your project. During this time, would you say your mood was consistently elevated—like you felt unusually confident, excited, or even irritable—and that this mood was different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Confident? Different from my usual self? Well, sure it's different, because I finally see what I'm actually capable of. It's not just confidence, Doc. Confidence is what you have when you think you can fix a leaky pipe or maybe change the spark plugs in your truck without messing it up. What I have right now is absolute certainty. It’s pure vision. \n\nI look at the work I'm doing... I look at that old Schiller Park house. You know the one, or maybe you don't, but it's this old property, just sitting there, and most people walk right by it like it's nothing. Just a pile of rotting wood and dust to them. But me? I honestly believe I can turn that old Schiller Park house into something important. I mean, truly important. Monumental. People are going to come see it. I know they will. They're going to travel, they'll be lining up all the way down the block just to witness it, because... well, because nobody understands light and pictures the way I do. \n\nNobody. You can take all these fancy architects and engineers with their framed diplomas—which I never needed, by the way, I left school in the tenth grade and I learned more working with my hands than they ever did sitting in a lecture hall—you can take all of them, and they still wouldn't see how the light is supposed to bend through the rooms. They don't see the pictures forming in the empty space. They just see drywall and two-by-fours. But I see the whole spectrum. I see how the natural light is going to interact with the frequencies and the wiring I'm laying down. \n\nSo, yes, I am excited! I am more than excited. I am a man who finally knows his true worth after seventy-three years of being asleep. I know that what I am building is going to change everything, and I just need to get it done. It's like I've been given the keys to the whole universe, so why wouldn't I feel on top of the world?\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least four days in a row where your mood was either extremely good—like euphoric or unusually high—or where you were easily irritated and it was noticeable to others?\n\nPatient: Four days? This... this absolute clarity, this incredible energy I'm telling you about, this has been going on for about three months now. She can't even get to the stove without stepping over rolls of insulation and heavy-gauge copper piping, and she complains about the dust every single morning. They just don't get it, and they don't want to get it. So no, no hospital, but it's getting mighty quiet around here when it comes to visitors. It's just me, the work, and Martha trying to navigate her way around a mountain of two-by-fours.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that others were misunderstanding or misinterpreting your actions, even when you believed you were completely clear and justified in what you were doing?\n\nPatient: Misinterpreting? Misunderstanding? Doc, that is the understatement of the century. That is exactly what I've been trying to tell you! I know people think I’m mixed up. I hear them talking. I hear Martha on the phone with her sister in the evenings, whispering in the hallway like I’m deaf just because I’m seventy-three. They practically crawl past my driveway, their engines just idling, taking it all in. Tom next door, he’s been out watering the exact same patch of dead grass for three weeks, just staring over the property line. Looking over at what I'm building, watching the progress. And old Mrs. Gable across the street? Her living room curtains are twitching every five minutes. I see her face in the glass. Little things like that—the trucks slowing down, the neighbors constantly looking over—it proves they know. I hear her saying, \"Oh, Frank’s lost the plot, Frank’s tearing the house to pieces for no reason.\" My brother, the guys from the plant... they all think I'm just some crazy old man who got a little too handy with a sledgehammer. \n\nBut I am not mixed up. Not even a little bit. What they don't grasp—what they absolutely refuse to wrap their heads around because they're so stuck in their little, ordinary lives—is that this isn't just a remodel. This entire neighborhood, this specific street right here in Schiller Park, it wasn't an accident that we ended up here. It was picked. Chosen. And I'm the one who was chosen to execute the vision. This house is going to be my last big civic art piece. A true public monument. \n\nAnd you want to know how I know I'm right? You want to know the absolute proof of it? Because the signs are everywhere. The people who are really paying attention, the ones who aren't blinded by jealousy or whatever my brother's problem is... they know it too. I see it every single day. I'll be out on the front lawn, maybe sorting through the copper pipes or organizing the drywall stacks, and I watch the street. The trucks—the municipal utility trucks, the delivery vans, even the heavy haulers—they don't just drive past at thirty miles an hour anymore. No, they slow way, way down. They practically crawl past my driveway, their engines just idling, taking it all in. Why else would a delivery truck hit the brakes in the middle of the block if they didn't know something incredibly important was happening here? \n\nAnd the neighbors! Oh, don't even get me started on the neighbors. They pretend they're just going about their normal routines, but I see them. Tom next door, he’s been out watering the exact same patch of dead grass for three weeks, just staring over the property line. Looking over at what I'm building, watching the progress. And old Mrs. Gable across the street? Her living room curtains are twitching every five minutes. I see her face in the glass. Little things like that—the trucks slowing down, the neighbors constantly looking over—it proves they know. They absolutely know this place was picked for this civic art piece, even if my own family is too blind to see the masterpiece I'm giving them. So let them misunderstand me. History is full of visionaries who were misunderstood by small-minded people!\n\nDoctor: After you stopped working intensely on the house project and things began to settle down, did you feel unusually sad, hopeless, or lose interest in almost all activities for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Stopped working? Settle down? Doc, I... I really don't know where you're getting this idea that things have settled down, or that I've stopped. I just told you, I haven't lost a single ounce of steam! But let's just say, hypothetically, that I had to pause—like, say, if I was waiting on a special order of heavy-duty brackets from the supply yard because the big-box stores just don't carry the right gauge for a project of this magnitude. Even then, sad? Hopeless? \n\nWhy in the world would I feel sad? You don't get sad when you're the one chosen to build something this important. I don't have the time or the inclination to sit around moping or staring at the wall. My mind is constantly going over the schematics, the angles, the way the morning light is going to hit the exposed beams once I finally get the roof line adjusted. Martha might be the one looking a little miserable these days because she can't watch her game shows in peace, but me? No. Absolutely not. \n\nI haven't had a single day—let alone two whole weeks—where I lost interest in what I'm doing. If anything, I'm more interested than I've ever been in my entire seventy-three years on this earth. I worked at that stamping plant for three decades, pulling levers and checking dies, and I never felt a fraction of the purpose I feel right now. So no, no sadness, no feeling down in the dumps. I don't know what you're fishing for with all this talk about hopelessness, but you are definitely casting your line in the wrong pond, Doc. There's no settling down happening here, and there's certainly no time to be sad.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Doc, I... I feel like we're going in circles here. Didn't I just answer this? I mean, I know you've got your clipboard and your checklist there, but you're asking me the exact same thing again. No. The answer is still no, not just about right now, but ever. In my entire life. I don't know why you're so stuck on this idea of me sitting around crying into my morning coffee or staring at a blank wall for two weeks straight. It's just not in my nature, and it never has been. 

Even going way, way back to when I was just a young buck starting out at the stamping plant—and let me tell you, that was grueling work, pulling double shifts, standing on those freezing concrete floors until your knees felt like they were packed with ground glass—even then, I wasn't sad. Exhausted? You bet. But empty? Hopeless? Never. I've always been a doer, a man of action. If a pipe bursts in the basement, you don't sit on the floor and weep about it for half a month, you get a pipe wrench, you turn off the main valve, and you fix it. That's the philosophy I've lived by for seventy-three years. 

Martha will tell you the exact same thing, if you ask her. I've never been one for the blues. Even when the plant went on that massive union strike back in '98, and we were scraping by, eating canned beans and franks six nights a week just to pay the mortgage... I didn't lose interest in things. I didn't get depressed. I used that time off to completely rebuild the carburetor on my old Chevy in the garage. I laid all the parts out on a tarp, cleaned every single valve, and had it purring like a kitten by the time the strike ended. 

So this whole two-week sadness thing you keep trying to pin on me? No. It's never happened. I just don't have the wiring for it. My brain is always moving, always planning the next step, always looking at how things fit together. I don't have a single empty day in my history, Doc, let alone fourteen of them in a row.

I hear her saying, \"Oh, Frank’s lost the plot, Frank’s tearing the house to pieces for no reason.\" My brother, the guys from the plant... they all think I'm just some crazy old man who got a little too handy with a sledgehammer. \n\nBut I am not mixed up. Not even a little bit. What they don't grasp—what they absolutely refuse to wrap their heads around because they're so stuck in their little, ordinary lives—is that this isn't just a remodel. This entire neighborhood, this specific street right here in Schiller Park, it wasn't an accident that we ended up here. It was picked. Chosen. And I'm the one who was chosen to execute the vision. This house is going to be my last big civic art piece. A true public monument. \n\nAnd you want to know how I know I'm right? You want to know the absolute proof of it? Because the signs are everywhere. The people who are really paying attention, the ones who aren't blinded by jealousy or whatever my brother's problem is... they know it too. I see it every single day. I'll be out on the front lawn, maybe sorting through the copper pipes or organizing the drywall stacks, and I watch the street. The trucks—the municipal utility trucks, the delivery vans, even the heavy haulers—they don't just drive past at thirty miles an hour anymore. No, they slow way, way down. They practically crawl past my driveway, their engines just idling, taking it all in. Why else would a delivery truck hit the brakes in the middle of the block if they didn't know something incredibly important was happening here? \n\nAnd the neighbors! Oh, don't even get me started on the neighbors. They pretend they're just going about their normal routines, but I see them. Tom next door, he’s been out watering the exact same patch of dead grass for three weeks, just staring over the property line. Looking over at what I'm building, watching the progress. And old Mrs. Gable across the street? Her living room curtains are twitching every five minutes. I see her face in the glass. Little things like that—the trucks slowing down, the neighbors constantly looking over—it proves they know. They absolutely know this place was picked for this civic art piece, even if my own family is too blind to see the masterpiece I'm giving them. So let them misunderstand me. History is full of visionaries who were misunderstood by small-minded people!\n\nDoctor: After you stopped working intensely on the house project and things began to settle down, did you feel unusually sad, hopeless, or lose interest in almost all activities for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Stopped working? Settle down? Doc, I... I really don't know where you're getting this idea that things have settled down, or that I've stopped. I just told you, I haven't lost a single ounce of steam! But let's just say, hypothetically, that I had to pause—like, say, if I was waiting on a special order of heavy-duty brackets from the supply yard because the big-box stores just don't carry the right gauge for a project of this magnitude. Even then, sad? Hopeless? \n\nWhy in the world would I feel sad? You don't get sad when you're the one chosen to build something this important. I don't have the time or the inclination to sit around moping or staring at the wall. My mind is constantly going over the schematics, the angles, the way the morning light is going to hit the exposed beams once I finally get the roof line adjusted. Martha might be the one looking a little miserable these days because she can't watch her game shows in peace, but me? No. Absolutely not. \n\nI haven't had a single day—let alone two whole weeks—where I lost interest in what I'm doing. If anything, I'm more interested than I've ever been in my entire seventy-three years on this earth. I worked at that stamping plant for three decades, pulling levers and checking dies, and I never felt a fraction of the purpose I feel right now. So no, no sadness, no feeling down in the dumps. I don't know what you're fishing for with all this talk about hopelessness, but you are definitely casting your line in the wrong pond, Doc. There's no settling down happening here, and there's certainly no time to be sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time—lasting at least two weeks—when you felt persistently sad, empty, or hopeless nearly every day, or lost interest or pleasure in almost all activities?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... I feel like we're going in circles here. Didn't I just answer this? I mean, I know you've got your clipboard and your checklist there, but you're asking me the exact same thing again. No. The answer is still no, not just about right now, but ever. In my entire life. I don't know why you're so stuck on this idea of me sitting around crying into my morning coffee or staring at a blank wall for two weeks straight. It's just not in my nature, and it never has been. \n\nEven going way, way back to when I was just a young buck starting out at the stamping plant—and let me tell you, that was grueling work, pulling double shifts, standing on those freezing concrete floors until your knees felt like they were packed with ground glass—even then, I wasn't sad. Exhausted? You bet. But empty? Hopeless? Never. I've always been a doer, a man of action. If a pipe bursts in the basement, you don't sit on the floor and weep about it for half a month, you get a pipe wrench, you turn off the main valve, and you fix it. That's the philosophy I've lived by for seventy-three years. \n\nMartha will tell you the exact same thing, if you ask her. I've never been one for the blues. Even when the plant went on that massive union strike back in '98, and we were scraping by, eating canned beans and franks six nights a week just to pay the mortgage... I didn't lose interest in things. I didn't get depressed. I used that time off to completely rebuild the carburetor on my old Chevy in the garage. I laid all the parts out on a tarp, cleaned every single valve, and had it purring like a kitten by the time the strike ended. \n\nSo this whole two-week sadness thing you keep trying to pin on me? No. It's never happened. I just don't have the wiring for it. My brain is always moving, always planning the next step, always looking at how things fit together. I don't have a single empty day in my history, Doc, let alone fourteen of them in a row.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore this recent period of intense energy and focus on the house project—going back any time in your life—have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt persistently down, sad, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Doc, are you... are you hard of hearing? Or maybe they teach you in medical school to just ask the exact same question three different ways to see if a guy trips up? Because I swear to you, we just covered this exact same territory not two minutes ago. Before the house project, ten years ago, fifty years ago... the answer is still a flat, absolute zero. 

I don't get down. I don't do the whole wallowing thing. I remember back in, oh, it must have been the winter of '79. The big blizzard. We were living over on Elm Street back then, before we moved to Schiller Park. The snow was piled up so high it broke the main beam on the carport, came crashing right down on my old Ford pickup. Martha was inside crying, saying we were ruined, the truck was totaled, the kids were freezing because the power lines snapped. Now, a normal guy might look at that and feel hopeless, right? Might get sad and lose interest in his life for a while. 

But me? I didn't spend two weeks crying about it. I didn't spend two *minutes* crying about it. I put on my heavy insulated coveralls—the green ones with the brass zippers I got from the army surplus store down on 4th Avenue, they don't even make them like that anymore, the zippers nowadays just pop right off if you pull too hard—and I went out there in three feet of snow. I spent fourteen hours straight jacking up that roof with a hydraulic lift I borrowed from my buddy Sal, pulling the truck out, and framing a temporary support out of some scrap four-by-fours. Sal said to me, "Frank, you're a machine, you don't ever stop," and he was right. I don't stop. 

So, going back to any time in my life, no. Never. I don't have these two-week periods of being sad. I don't lose interest in things. I am always on the move, always fixing, always building, always figuring out the next problem. I really don't know how many different ways I can explain this to you, Doc. You keep digging for this sadness, but the hole is empty. There's just nothing down there for you to find.

They actually tried to tell me to stop! Can you imagine? Some guy making twenty bucks an hour trying to tell me about structural integrity when he doesn't even know which way is true north. Depressed? Doc, I don't... I really don't think you're grasping the sheer volume of purpose that's running through me right now. How could I possibly feel down? Down is for people who are disconnected. It’s for people who don't have a mission. Like... well, like my brother-in-law, Gary. Gary sits around all day in his undershirt watching golf on the television, just looking like a deflated balloon. Now *that* is depressed. \n\nBut me? No, no, no, absolutely not. I haven't lost interest in a single thing! If anything, I am profoundly, deeply interested in *everything*. Just pure, unbroken focus.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period like this before, where you felt extremely energetic and driven to work on big projects, maybe even so intense that someone suggested you needed help or should slow down?\n\nPatient: Before? Like this? No, no, absolutely not. You see, Doc, you don't just... you don't just get struck by lightning every Tuesday. This is a singular event. I mean, I have been around a long time. Seventy-three years is a lot of days, a lot of mornings waking up and putting your boots on. And sure, I have worked hard in my life. When I was younger, back after I left school and started working, and then later when I was pulling double shifts down at the stamping plant, I would work fifty, sixty hours a week. I would come home smelling like machine oil and metal shavings, and Martha would tell me Frank, you are working too hard, you need to rest. But that was just survival. That was just paying the mortgage and keeping the lights on. It was just moving meat and bones around. It was not this. \n\nThis is not just energy. It is not like I drank too much coffee or something. It is a direct connection to the source. I never felt the vibrations before September. I never saw the grid. I lived my whole life completely blind to the frequencies, just like everybody else, just walking around, eating, sleeping, watching the evening news, completely oblivious to the fact that the very foundation of the world was humming right beneath my feet. \n\nSo no, I have not had a period like this before because the universe was not ready before. And as for people telling me to get help, well, like I said, Martha used to tell me to take a load off, maybe sit down and watch a ballgame. But she never looked at me like I was a stranger before this. She never called my daughter crying and saying I was out of my mind until now. They just do not understand that I am finally awake. Why would I need help for finally opening my eyes?\n\nDoctor: Doctor: During this time when you were so intensely focused on the house project, did you notice that your thoughts were racing so fast that it was hard to keep up, or that ideas were flowing so quickly that you couldn't organize them?\n\nPatient: Racing? No, no, no. You've got it all wrong, Doc. Racing implies chaos. Racing implies things are out of control, like a car with no brakes careening down a steep hill, just waiting to crash at the bottom. That's not what's happening up here. Not at all. If anything, my thoughts are... they are crystal clear. They are perfectly, immaculately organized. It's like someone unrolled a massive, beautiful blueprint right across the front of my mind and turned on a spotlight. \n\nI don't struggle to keep up with them because they aren't running away from me. They are marching. Marching in perfect unison. For instance, just the other morning, I was looking at the junction box in the basement—the old one, the one with the frayed wires from 1978 that the original contractor just slapped together like a complete amateur. And in the span of maybe three seconds, I saw exactly what needed to be done. I saw the pathway the current needed to take, I saw the exact angle I needed to bend the copper wire—45 degrees, mind you, not 40, not 50, because 45 degrees aligns with the natural rotation of the earth's axis—and I saw exactly where the quartz needed to be placed to filter the interference from the smart meters down the street. \n\nThat's not disorganized! That is precision. That is absolute, undeniable clarity. It’s like... well, it’s like when I used to work at the stamping plant, right? You had the line, and the line moved at a specific speed, and every part had to be in the exact right place at the exact right time or the whole machine jammed. My mind is the perfect machine right now. There's no jamming. Every idea slots into the next one seamlessly. \n\nMartha says to me, she stands there with her arms crossed and says, \"Frank, you're talking so fast, you're jumping all over the place, I can't follow a word you're saying.\" But I'm not jumping! I'm just connecting point A to point Z while she's still trying to figure out point B. Just because she can't see the lines connecting everything doesn't mean the lines aren't there. It just means she doesn't have the vision for it. So no, my thoughts aren't racing and they certainly aren't messy. They're just finally operating at the correct voltage, and I know exactly where every single one of them is going.\n\nDoctor: During this time working on the house project, did you feel unusually good, like you were on top of the world or had a special connection to everything around you?\n\nPatient: Good? Good doesn't even begin to scratch the surface, Doc. I feel... well, most days I feel completely charged up. Just humming with it! It’s almost this profound sense of... of joy, really. It’s like I’m young again. I wake up before the sun is even thinking about coming up, and my joints don't ache, my back doesn't bother me from all those years on the concrete floor at the stamping plant. I feel twenty-five years old, practically leaping out of bed because I have this incredible purpose and I’m just so deeply connected to the work. It’s a beautiful, beautiful feeling to finally be alive like that, to feel the whole world just vibrating right along with you. \n\nBut... and this is the sticking point, you see... when you have that much current running through you, you can't have people stepping on the wires. It causes a short circuit. So if Martha comes down the stairs with her arms crossed, questioning why I had to pull up the kitchen linoleum, or if those so-called \"professional\" contractors I brought in for the heavy lifting try to tell me my layout isn't up to their little city code... well, I get hot. \n\nI get hot fast. I mean, my blood just boils right up the back of my neck because they are standing in the way of the grid! When they start questioning me, asking these small-minded, ignorant questions about permits and drywall, I just... I start yelling. I can't even stop myself. I have to shout just to get them to back off and let me do what needs to be done. They just stand there looking at me like I'm the crazy one, but they're the ones trying to stop a freight train with a stop sign! So yes, I feel on top of the world, absolutely joyful, but if my wife or these contractors come around trying to put out my fire, I am going to yell until they get out of my way. You'd yell too if you were building a masterpiece and someone kept trying to hand you a broom!\n\nDoctor: Doctor: So, you mentioned feeling extremely energetic, driven, and connected to your project. During this time, would you say your mood was consistently elevated—like you felt unusually confident, excited, or even irritable—and that this mood was different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: Confident? Different from my usual self? Well, sure it's different, because I finally see what I'm actually capable of. It's not just confidence, Doc. Confidence is what you have when you think you can fix a leaky pipe or maybe change the spark plugs in your truck without messing it up. What I have right now is absolute certainty. It’s pure vision. \n\nI look at the work I'm doing... I look at that old Schiller Park house. You know the one, or maybe you don't, but it's this old property, just sitting there, and most people walk right by it like it's nothing. Just a pile of rotting wood and dust to them. But me? I honestly believe I can turn that old Schiller Park house into something important. I mean, truly important. Monumental. People are going to come see it. I know they will. They're going to travel, they'll be lining up all the way down the block just to witness it, because... well, because nobody understands light and pictures the way I do. \n\nNobody. You can take all these fancy architects and engineers with their framed diplomas—which I never needed, by the way, I left school in the tenth grade and I learned more working with my hands than they ever did sitting in a lecture hall—you can take all of them, and they still wouldn't see how the light is supposed to bend through the rooms. They don't see the pictures forming in the empty space. They just see drywall and two-by-fours. But I see the whole spectrum. I see how the natural light is going to interact with the frequencies and the wiring I'm laying down. \n\nSo, yes, I am excited! I am more than excited. I am a man who finally knows his true worth after seventy-three years of being asleep. I know that what I am building is going to change everything, and I just need to get it done. It's like I've been given the keys to the whole universe, so why wouldn't I feel on top of the world?\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period lasting at least four days in a row where your mood was either extremely good—like euphoric or unusually high—or where you were easily irritated and it was noticeable to others?\n\nPatient: Four days? Doc, you're joking, right? Four days is a drop in the bucket. That’s a weekend project for some amateur watching a home improvement show on television. We are way, way past four days. This... this absolute clarity, this incredible energy I'm telling you about, this has been going on for about three months now. Three solid months! \n\nIt all really kicked into high gear right when the remodeling started. And I don't mean just slapping on a coat of paint or fixing a squeaky hinge, I mean when I really got into the meat of it and everything in the house got turned completely upside down. I mean, you should see the place right now. It is literally upside down! I had to pull up the oak flooring in the living room—beautiful wood, sure, but it was completely blocking the grounding path to the basement—and now I've got stacks of it sitting right in the middle of Martha’s kitchen. She can't even get to the stove without stepping over rolls of insulation and heavy-gauge copper piping, and she complains about the dust every single morning. She says, \"Frank, I can't live in a construction zone!\" \n\nBut you have to tear down the old to build the new, don't you? You have to turn it all upside down to see the real foundation, to understand how the house breathes. So if you're asking if I've felt this good, this driven, for four days... well, you need to expand your timeline, Doc. It's been a quarter of a year. Ninety straight days of absolute perfection since I started tearing that house apart to build my vision, and I haven't lost an ounce of steam yet.\n\nDoctor: During this time when you were so intensely focused on the house project, did you ever need to be hospitalized or did anyone try to intervene because they were worried about your behavior?\n\nPatient: Hospitalized? Let's not get carried away here, Doc. Nobody is hauling me off in a white coat just because I actually have a work ethic and a vision. But as for people stepping in, or being worried... well, I suppose you could say things have gotten a bit strained on the home front. \n\nI’ll be the first to admit, the house has gotten pretty hard for my wife and me to actually live in on a day-to-day basis. I mean, it’s a full-blown construction zone, like I was telling you. My materials—the heavy-gauge wire, the drywall stacks, the PVC pipes—and all these half-finished setups are just completely taking over the rooms we need to use every single day. Take the downstairs bathroom, for example. I had to rip the vanity and the sink completely out to get to the main plumbing stack because the frequencies need a pure ground to the earth, so now if you need to wash your hands or use the toilet, you have to hike all the way up the stairs. And the living room? Martha can't even sit in her favorite armchair to watch her evening programs because I’ve got it covered with a tarp, my blueprints, and a portable table saw. She’s upset, naturally, and she tells me about it every morning over coffee.\n\nBut the part that really gets my goat, the part that's actually frustrating, is how the people I care about are treating me. They come over, they start poking their noses in, asking why there's a hole in the dining room ceiling or why I haven't slept, and then the arguments start. And I’ve already told you what happens when they get in my way and try to stop the train—I yell. I have to make them understand! But now? Now my own brother won't even return my phone calls after we got into a massive shouting match last Sunday over the load-bearing supports on the porch. My old buddies from the stamping plant, guys I've known for thirty years, they just stopped coming around entirely. They're all avoiding me after these arguments. They just don't get it, and they don't want to get it. So no, no hospital, but it's getting mighty quiet around here when it comes to visitors. It's just me, the work, and Martha trying to navigate her way around a mountain of two-by-fours.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that others were misunderstanding or misinterpreting your actions, even when you believed you were completely clear and justified in what you were doing?\n\nPatient: Misinterpreting? Misunderstanding? Doc, that is the understatement of the century. That is exactly what I've been trying to tell you! I know people think I’m mixed up. I hear them talking. I hear Martha on the phone with her sister in the evenings, whispering in the hallway like I’m deaf just because I’m seventy-three. I hear her saying, \"Oh, Frank’s lost the plot, Frank’s tearing the house to pieces for no reason.\" My brother, the guys from the plant... they all think I'm just some crazy old man who got a little too handy with a sledgehammer. \n\nBut I am not mixed up. Not even a little bit. What they don't grasp—what they absolutely refuse to wrap their heads around because they're so stuck in their little, ordinary lives—is that this isn't just a remodel. This entire neighborhood, this specific street right here in Schiller Park, it wasn't an accident that we ended up here. It was picked. Chosen. And I'm the one who was chosen to execute the vision. This house is going to be my last big civic art piece. A true public monument. \n\nAnd you want to know how I know I'm right? You want to know the absolute proof of it? Because the signs are everywhere. The people who are really paying attention, the ones who aren't blinded by jealousy or whatever my brother's problem is... they know it too. I see it every single day. I'll be out on the front lawn, maybe sorting through the copper pipes or organizing the drywall stacks, and I watch the street. The trucks—the municipal utility trucks, the delivery vans, even the heavy haulers—they don't just drive past at thirty miles an hour anymore. No, they slow way, way down. They practically crawl past my driveway, their engines just idling, taking it all in. Why else would a delivery truck hit the brakes in the middle of the block if they didn't know something incredibly important was happening here? \n\nAnd the neighbors! Oh, don't even get me started on the neighbors. They pretend they're just going about their normal routines, but I see them. Tom next door, he’s been out watering the exact same patch of dead grass for three weeks, just staring over the property line. Looking over at what I'm building, watching the progress. And old Mrs. Gable across the street? Her living room curtains are twitching every five minutes. I see her face in the glass. Little things like that—the trucks slowing down, the neighbors constantly looking over—it proves they know. They absolutely know this place was picked for this civic art piece, even if my own family is too blind to see the masterpiece I'm giving them. So let them misunderstand me. History is full of visionaries who were misunderstood by small-minded people!\n\nDoctor: After you stopped working intensely on the house project and things began to settle down, did you feel unusually sad, hopeless, or lose interest in almost all activities for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Stopped working? Settle down? Doc, I... I really don't know where you're getting this idea that things have settled down, or that I've stopped. I just told you, I haven't lost a single ounce of steam! But let's just say, hypothetically, that I had to pause—like, say, if I was waiting on a special order of heavy-duty brackets from the supply yard because the big-box stores just don't carry the right gauge for a project of this magnitude. If anything, I'm more interested than I've ever been in my entire seventy-three years on this earth. I worked at that stamping plant for three decades, pulling levers and checking dies, and I never felt a fraction of the purpose I feel right now. So no, no sadness, no feeling down in the dumps. I don't know what you're fishing for with all this talk about hopelessness, but you are definitely casting your line in the wrong pond, Doc. There's no settling down happening here, and there's certainly no time to be sad.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of time—lasting at least two weeks—when you felt persistently sad, empty, or hopeless nearly every day, or lost interest or pleasure in almost all activities?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... Exhausted? You bet. But empty? Hopeless? Never. I've always been a doer, a man of action. That's the philosophy I've lived by for seventy-three years. \n\nMartha will tell you the exact same thing, if you ask her. I've never been one for the blues. Even when the plant went on that massive union strike back in '98, and we were scraping by, eating canned beans and franks six nights a week just to pay the mortgage... I didn't lose interest in things. I didn't get depressed. I used that time off to completely rebuild the carburetor on my old Chevy in the garage. I laid all the parts out on a tarp, cleaned every single valve, and had it purring like a kitten by the time the strike ended. \n\nSo this whole two-week sadness thing you keep trying to pin on me? No. It's never happened. I just don't have the wiring for it. My brain is always moving, always planning the next step, always looking at how things fit together. I don't have a single empty day in my history, Doc, let alone fourteen of them in a row.\n\nDoctor: Before this recent period of intense energy and focus on the house project—going back any time in your life—have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt persistently down, sad, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Doc, are you... are you hard of hearing? Or maybe they teach you in medical school to just ask the exact same question three different ways to see if a guy trips up? Because I swear to you, we just covered this exact same territory not two minutes ago. Before the house project, ten years ago, fifty years ago... the answer is still a flat, absolute zero. \n\nI don't get down. I don't do the whole wallowing thing. I remember back in, oh, it must have been the winter of '79. The big blizzard. We were living over on Elm Street back then, before we moved to Schiller Park. The snow was piled up so high it broke the main beam on the carport, came crashing right down on my old Ford pickup. Martha was inside crying, saying we were ruined, the truck was totaled, the kids were freezing because the power lines snapped. Now, a normal guy might look at that and feel hopeless, right? I spent fourteen hours straight jacking up that roof with a hydraulic lift I borrowed from my buddy Sal, pulling the truck out, and framing a temporary support out of some scrap four-by-fours. Sal said to me, \"Frank, you're a machine, you don't ever stop,\" and he was right. I don't stop. \n\nSo, going back to any time in my life, no. Never. I don't have these two-week periods of being sad. I don't lose interest in things. I am always on the move, always fixing, always building, always figuring out the next problem. I really don't know how many different ways I can explain this to you, Doc. You keep digging for this sadness, but the hole is empty. There's just nothing down there for you to find.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
